=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a binary string s of length n, where:

'1' represents an active section.
'0' represents an inactive section.

You can perform at most one trade to maximize the number of active sections in s. In a trade, you:

Convert a contiguous block of '1's that is surrounded by '0's to all '0's.
Afterward, convert a contiguous block of '0's that is surrounded by '1's to all '1's.

Return the maximum number of active sections in s after making the optimal trade.
Note: Treat s as if it is augmented with a '1' at both ends, forming t = '1' + s + '1'. The augmented '1's do not contribute to the final count.
 
Example 1:

Input: s = "01"
Output: 1
Explanation:
Because there is no block of '1's surrounded by '0's, no valid trade is possible. The maximum number of active sections is 1.

Example 2:

Input: s = "0100"
Output: 4
Explanation:

String "0100" → Augmented to "101001".
Choose "0100", convert "101001" → "100001" → "111111".
The final string without augmentation is "1111". The maximum number of active sections is 4.


Example 3:

Input: s = "1000100"
Output: 7
Explanation:

String "1000100" → Augmented to "110001001".
Choose "000100", convert "110001001" → "110000001" → "111111111".
The final string without augmentation is "1111111". The maximum number of active sections is 7.


Example 4:

Input: s = "01010"
Output: 4
Explanation:

String "01010" → Augmented to "1010101".
Choose "010", convert "1010101" → "1000101" → "1111101".
The final string without augmentation is "11110". The maximum number of active sections is 4.


 
Constraints:

1 <= n == s.length <= 10^5
s[i] is either '0' or '1'

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maxActiveSectionsAfterTrade(self, s: str) -> int:
        
```

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:

- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

The solution maybe: count ones, then split by '1', compute zeros lengths. Answer = ones + max(zero_left + zero_right for each '1' segment? Actually I remember seeing code:
```
class Solution:
    def maxActiveSectionsAfterTrade(self, s: str) -> int:
        ones = s.count('1')
        zeros = []
        cnt = 0
        for c in s:
            if c == '0':
                cnt += 1
            else:
                if cnt:
                    zeros.append(cnt)
                    cnt = 0
        if cnt:
            zeros.append(cnt)
        if len(zeros) < 2:
            return ones
        return ones + max(zeros[i] + zeros[i+1] for i in range(len(zeros)-1))
```

Example1 s="01": zero runs [1], len<2 => ones=1. This formula gives answer ones + max sum of adjacent zero runs. It doesn't explicitly check one-run target. Is it always valid? Let's recall exact LeetCode problem maybe "3228. Maximum Number of Operations to Move Ones to the End" no. There is LeetCode 3228 "Maximum Number of Operations to Move Ones to the End" maybe different. "Maximum Active Sections After Trade" is LeetCode 3229? I remember a problem from LeetCode Weekly Contest 407? The solution indeed:
```
class Solution:
    def maxActiveSectionsAfterTrade(self, s: str) -> int:
        ones = s.count('1')
        zeros = []
        cnt = 0
        for c in s:
            if c == '0':
                cnt += 1
            else:
                if cnt:
                    zeros.append(cnt)
                    cnt = 0
        if cnt:
            zeros.append(cnt)
        if len(zeros) < 2:
            return ones
        return ones + max(zeros[i] + zeros[i+1] for i in range(len(zeros)-1))
```
But wait, I recall some solutions also consider "1" blocks and "0" blocks with augmented boundaries, and answer = ones + max(zeros[i] + zeros[i+1])? Let's verify with LeetCode constraints and examples. Example 2 "0100" output 4. Example 3 "1000100" output 7. Example 4 "01010" output 4. Yes.

ans = s.count('1')
cnt = 0
zero = []

    if ch == '0':
        cnt += 1
    else:
        if cnt:
            zero.append(cnt)
            cnt = 0
if cnt:
    zero.append(cnt)
for i in range(len(zero)-1):
    ans = max(ans, s.count('1') + zero[i] + zero[i+1])
return ans
```

Let's maybe search memory of LeetCode problem "3228. Maximum Number of Operations to Move Ones to the End" no. I recall a problem "Maximum Active Sections After Trade" from LeetCode 3229? The solution in C++:
```
int maxActiveSectionsAfterTrade(string s) {
    int ones = count(s.begin(), s.end(), '1');
    vector<int> zeros;
    int cnt=0;
    for(char c: s) {
        if(c=='0') cnt++;
        else {
            if(cnt) zeros.push_back(cnt), cnt=0;
        }
    }
    if(cnt) zeros.push_back(cnt);
    if(zeros.size()<2) return ones;
    int mx=0;
    for(int i=0;i+1<zeros.size();i++) mx=max(mx, zeros[i]+zeros[i+1]);
    return ones+mx;
}
```
Yes.

Let's maybe formulate code with O(1) memory:
```
class Solution:
    def maxActiveSectionsAfterTrade(self, s: str) -> int:
        ones = s.count('1')
        zero_runs = []
        cnt = 0
        for ch in s:
            if ch == '0':
                cnt += 1
            else:
                if cnt:
                    zero_runs.append(cnt)
                    cnt = 0
        if cnt:
            zero_runs.append(cnt)
        if len(zero_runs) < 2:
            return ones
        max_gain = 0
        for i in range(len(zero_runs)-1):
            max_gain = max(max_gain, zero_runs[i] + zero_runs[i+1])
        return ones + max_gain
```
This is fine. Could avoid list:
```
prev = -1
max_gain = 0
cnt = 0
for ch in s:
    if ch == '0':
        cnt += 1
    else:
        if cnt:
            if prev != -1:
                max_gain = max(max_gain, prev + cnt)
            prev = cnt
            cnt = 0
if cnt:
    if prev != -1:
        max_gain = max(max_gain, prev + cnt)
return ones + max_gain
```
But list is clearer. n=1e5, list okay. Need output code only. Use list.

After first conversion, zero runs i-1 and i merge with target into a zero block M of length L = z[i-1] + one_i + z[i]. Other zero runs remain. If we choose M, gain = z[i-1]+z[i]. If we choose another zero run j, gain = z[j] - one_i. Could this be > max adjacent sum? Let's attempt to construct a counterexample. Let zero runs [100, 1, 100]? ones count maybe 3? So any zero run can be included in some trade with at least one adjacent zero run. Therefore the maximum over all trades that choose merged zero block is at least max_j (z[j] + z[neighbor]) >= z[j] + 1. Alternative net = z[j] - one_i <= z[j] -1 < z[j]+1. So alternative cannot beat. What if one_i = 0? impossible. So merged always better. Good.

Let's consider if the problem might be from LeetCode and expects method signature exactly. We'll provide class Solution with method. No imports.

Let's maybe think of a more efficient O(1) memory code that still clear:
```
class Solution:
    def maxActiveSectionsAfterTrade(self, s: str) -> int:
        ones = s.count('1')
        zero_groups = []
        cur = 0
        for ch in s:
            if ch == '0':
                cur += 1
            elif cur:
                zero_groups.append(cur)
                cur = 0
        if cur:
            zero_groups.append(cur)
        if len(zero_groups) < 2:
            return ones
        return ones + max(zero_groups[i] + zero_groups[i+1] for i in range(len(zero_groups)-1))
```
This is final.

But let's not ignore the "commit-implement-recheck" instruction. It says reason in a commit-implement-recheck way. But final output only code. We can include code only. Need not include explanation. But maybe they expect final answer with code block. We'll do that.

Before final, let's double-check with examples using code mentally:
Example1 s="01": ones=1. zero_groups: ch0 cur1; ch1 append1 cur0; end cur0. len1 -> return1.
Example2 "0100": ones=1. zero_groups [1,2]. max(3)=3. return4.
Example3 "1000100": ones=2. zero_groups: ch1 cur0; ch0 cur1,2,3; ch1 append3; ch0 cur1,2; end append2. [3,2], max5, return7.
Example4 "01010": ones=2. zero_groups [1,1,1], max2, return4. Good.

Let's consider if there is a possibility to get answer > n? No. Formula ones + max sum of two zero runs. Since ones + all zero runs = n. Max sum of two zero runs <= total zeros, so answer <= ones+total zeros = n. Good.

Let's think if there is a case with zero_groups length >=2 but max_gain maybe 0? No, each zero run length >=1, sum >=2. So answer > ones.

Let's think if the "trade" could be performed on augmented t and then final count excludes augmented ones, but what if the second step converts a zero block that includes the augmented ones? It cannot because zero block contains only zeros. Good.

Let's maybe consider if the first step can convert a one block that includes augmented ones? It would not be surrounded by zeros because augmented one at boundary has no zero outside. But what if the one block is at the beginning of t and the "outside" is considered a '0' because augmented? No, augmented is '1'. The note says augmented with '1' at both ends, so outside is not considered. So no.

Let's think if there is any nuance in "surrounded by '0's" for a block at the very beginning of t? It has no character before; not surrounded. Good.

Now, let's maybe explore a more complex scenario to ensure zero-run pair sum is exactly the gain, not maybe plus target one length? Wait, after second conversion, the merged zero block includes the target one run. When converted to ones, the target one run becomes ones. It was ones originally, so no net change for target. The adjacent zero runs become ones, net gain. So gain is adjacent zero runs only. If one mistakenly thought gain includes target length, would overcount. Example2 target length1, gain3 not4. Formula ones1+3=4. If included target, ones1+1+3=5 >n. So no.

Let's think if there is any scenario where the target one run is not restored because the second step chooses a different zero block? Then target remains zeros, net loss. But optimal chooses merged. Good.

Let's consider if after first conversion, the merged zero block might not be the only zero block surrounded by ones, but it's the best. We already reasoned.

Let's maybe think if there is a way to perform two trades? "at most one trade" no.

Let's think if the problem constraints n up to 1e5, recursion not needed.

Let's maybe consider if the method should handle empty string? Constraints n>=1, no.

Let's think if there is any issue with Python class indentation. Final code:
```python
class Solution:
    def maxActiveSectionsAfterTrade(self, s: str) -> int:
        ones = s.count('1')
        zero_runs = []
        cur = 0
        for ch in s:
            if ch == '0':
                cur += 1
            else:
                if cur:
                    zero_runs.append(cur)
                    cur = 0
        if cur:
            zero_runs.append(cur)

        if len(zero_runs) < 2:
            return ones

        max_gain = 0
        for i in range(len(zero_runs) - 1):
            max_gain = max(max_gain, zero_runs[i] + zero_runs[i + 1])

        return ones + max_gain
```
This is final.

    else:
        if cur:

                best = max(best, prev + cur)

            cur = 0

    if prev != -1:

Let's think if there is any possible trade where the first block of ones is an internal one run but the second step chooses a zero block that is not the merged one and yields a net gain equal to original ones + zero_run_j - one_i. We argued not optimal. But let's prove with max adjacent sum:
Let z[0..k-1]. Any valid trade must choose a target one run between some z[p] and z[p+1] (because target surrounded by zeros). After first conversion, the zero runs available include M = z[p] + target_len + z[p+1], and all other z[q]. If choose M, final = O + z[p] + z[p+1]. If choose z[q] where q not p,p+1, final = O - target_len + z[q]. Since target_len >=1, this is <= O + z[q] -1. Since k>=2, z[q] has at least one neighbor z[r] (if q=0, r=1; if q=k-1, r=k-2; else either). The trade targeting the one run between z[min(q,r)] and z[max(q,r)] gives gain z[q]+z[r] >= z[q]+1. So max adjacent sum >= z[q]+1 > z[q]-1 >= alternative. Thus merged is always at least as good. Good.

What if q is adjacent to p but not p+1? If q = p-1, then q is adjacent to target? Actually if target between p and p+1, adjacent zero runs are p and p+1. q=p-1 is not adjacent to target; it's separated by one run between p-1 and p. Alternative net = z[p-1] - target_len. The trade targeting one run between p-1 and p gives gain z[p-1]+z[p] >= z[p-1]+1 > alternative. Good. So formula.

Thus final solution solid.
